Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… they told me I needed to come in after what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I know how the bay is supposed to feel when things are in the right place, you know? The airway bags, the jump kits, the numbers on the dispatch board—there’s a kind of order to it.

I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern that made sense to me. It wasn’t random. I had to get them lined up the right way, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, because if I stopped before it was finished it felt like something in the whole bay would go wrong. Like the sequence would break.

My lieutenant told me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I mean, I heard him, I knew he was talking to me, but stepping away felt… unsafe, like I’d be leaving it wrong. So I kept doing it, and I guess that’s why they sent me here.

I don’t know… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, I don’t think. Like, I’m tired a lot, especially with the shifts, and sometimes my head won’t shut up after a call or after I’ve been trying to get things lined up right, but I wouldn’t say I feel energized in a good way.

The sleep thing is hard to answer because EMS sleep is already messed up. You get used to sleeping in chunks, waking up fast, always kind of listening. So if you ask whether I’ve needed less sleep and felt great, like I could just keep going and didn’t need it—no, I don’t think so. If I’m not sleeping, it doesn’t feel like extra energy. It feels more like I can’t settle.

Racing thoughts… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It’s more like something keeps repeating or I’m trying to hold the order of things in my head, not like I’m full of ideas or feeling unstoppable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed periods like that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, not like that. But the believing something part—yes. I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but it’s not just a feeling.

At the station, we have cameras everywhere, obviously, for safety and liability and all that. I know the injury review people have access, and some of the supervisors too, and I’m pretty sure they’ve been watching me through those cameras. Not just reviewing after something happens—watching. Like checking what I’m doing, when I’m writing, where I’m standing, whether I’m near the computer or the charts.

And the triage notes… that’s the part people don’t want to talk about. I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, corrections, documentation cleanup, but that doesn’t make sense when the changes all point in the same direction. It’s like they’re building a file on me.

I’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed periods like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things that others around you didn’t seem to notice—like hearing voices, seeing things that others couldn’t see, or believing something strongly that others didn’t share?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, not like that. I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but it’s not just a feeling.\n\nAt the station, we have cameras everywhere, obviously, for safety and liability and all that. I know the injury review people have access, and some of the supervisors too, and I’m pretty sure they’ve been watching me through those cameras. Not just reviewing after something happens—watching. Like checking what I’m doing, when I’m writing, where I’m standing, whether I’m near the computer or the charts.\n\nAnd the triage notes… that’s the part people don’t want to talk about. I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, corrections, documentation cleanup, but that doesn’t make sense when the changes all point in the same direction. It’s like they’re building a file on me.\n\nI’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me more about how you know the changes to your triage notes are being made with the intent to target you? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.

Like, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.

And when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.

And the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. And I know how it sounds, but I also know what I’m seeing.

I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, corrections, documentation cleanup, but that doesn’t make sense when the changes all point in the same direction. It’s like they’re building a file on me.\n\nI’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the changes to your triage notes are being made with the intent to target you? Do you believe someone has told you this directly, or have you come to this conclusion based on patterns you’ve noticed?\n\nPatient: No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.\n\nAnd when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.\n\nAnd the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. And I know how it sounds, but I also know what I’m seeing.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me if you believe these changes to your notes and being watched by supervisors are happening because someone is deliberately trying to harm or undermine you, or do you think it might be due to other factors like misunderstanding or routine procedures?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s just misunderstanding. I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, “Maybe it’s routine procedures, maybe you’re stressed, maybe you’re taking it personally.” But if it were routine, it would affect everyone the same way, or at least it would look random. This doesn’t look random.

It feels deliberate. I’m not saying somebody is going to jump out from behind a door or anything like that, but yes, I think they’re trying to undermine me. Harm me professionally, I guess. Make me look unsafe, unstable, careless—whatever word they need in the file later. Because that’s how these things work. Nobody says it directly. They just build the paper trail until you can’t defend yourself anymore.

The cameras are part of it because they’re watching how I move around the station, when I’m writing, whether I’m near the computer, what I’m doing after calls. And then the notes change, or comments get made, and it lines up too closely. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep landing on me in a way that makes me look bad.

So… yes. I think someone, or more than one person, is doing it on purpose. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.

But the believing something part—yes. I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but it’s not just a feeling.\n\nAt the station, we have cameras everywhere, obviously, for safety and liability and all that. I know the injury review people have access, and some of the supervisors too, and I’m pretty sure they’ve been watching me through those cameras. Not just reviewing after something happens—watching. Like checking what I’m doing, when I’m writing, where I’m standing, whether I’m near the computer or the charts.\n\nAnd the triage notes… that’s the part people don’t want to talk about. I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, corrections, documentation cleanup, but that doesn’t make sense when the changes all point in the same direction. It’s like they’re building a file on me.\n\nI’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the changes to your triage notes are being made with the intent to target you? Do you believe someone has told you this directly, or have you come to this conclusion based on patterns you’ve noticed?\n\nPatient: No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.\n\nAnd when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.\n\nAnd the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. And I know how it sounds, but I also know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe these changes to your notes and being watched by supervisors are happening because someone is deliberately trying to harm or undermine you, or do you think it might be due to other factors like misunderstanding or routine procedures?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just misunderstanding. I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, “Maybe it’s routine procedures, maybe you’re stressed, maybe you’re taking it personally.” But if it were routine, it would affect everyone the same way, or at least it would look random. This doesn’t look random.\n\nIt feels deliberate. I’m not saying somebody is going to jump out from behind a door or anything like that, but yes, I think they’re trying to undermine me. Harm me professionally, I guess. Make me look unsafe, unstable, careless—whatever word they need in the file later. Because that’s how these things work. Nobody says it directly. They just build the paper trail until you can’t defend yourself anymore.\n\nThe cameras are part of it because they’re watching how I move around the station, when I’m writing, whether I’m near the computer, what I’m doing after calls. And then the notes change, or comments get made, and it lines up too closely. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep landing on me in a way that makes me look bad.\n\nSo… yes. I think someone, or more than one person, is doing it on purpose. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt like your thoughts are not entirely your own—like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking them out?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. Not like… someone putting a thought in my head or pulling one out. I’ve never had it where I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m like, “That thought wasn’t mine,” if that’s what you mean.

I mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, and I’ll go over stuff again and again, especially when something doesn’t add up. But that still feels like me thinking it. It’s my own mind trying to make sense of what’s happening. It’s not like an outside voice or some force reaching in and changing my thoughts.

Sometimes people make me feel like I’m not allowed to trust my own thinking, if that makes sense. Like they’ll say I’m misreading something or that I’m stressed, and then I start second-guessing how to explain it. But the thoughts themselves feel like mine. So no, I wouldn’t say anyone is putting them there or taking them away.

It’s like they’re building a file on me.\n\nI’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the changes to your triage notes are being made with the intent to target you? Do you believe someone has told you this directly, or have you come to this conclusion based on patterns you’ve noticed?\n\nPatient: No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.\n\nAnd when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.\n\nAnd the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. And I know how it sounds, but I also know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe these changes to your notes and being watched by supervisors are happening because someone is deliberately trying to harm or undermine you, or do you think it might be due to other factors like misunderstanding or routine procedures?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just misunderstanding. I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, “Maybe it’s routine procedures, maybe you’re stressed, maybe you’re taking it personally.” But if it were routine, it would affect everyone the same way, or at least it would look random. This doesn’t look random.\n\nIt feels deliberate. I’m not saying somebody is going to jump out from behind a door or anything like that, but yes, I think they’re trying to undermine me. Harm me professionally, I guess. Make me look unsafe, unstable, careless—whatever word they need in the file later. Because that’s how these things work. Nobody says it directly. They just build the paper trail until you can’t defend yourself anymore.\n\nThe cameras are part of it because they’re watching how I move around the station, when I’m writing, whether I’m near the computer, what I’m doing after calls. And then the notes change, or comments get made, and it lines up too closely. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep landing on me in a way that makes me look bad.\n\nSo… yes. I think someone, or more than one person, is doing it on purpose. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not entirely your own—like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking them out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… someone putting a thought in my head or pulling one out. I’ve never had it where I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m like, “That thought wasn’t mine,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, and I’ll go over stuff again and again, especially when something doesn’t add up. But that still feels like me thinking it. It’s my own mind trying to make sense of what’s happening. Yes. That’s actually a lot of what I’ve been trying to explain. It’s not like every random thing in the room feels aimed at me, but certain things at work do, because they connect together in a way other people either don’t notice or don’t want to admit.

Like the station cameras. Other people act like they’re just there, like background equipment, but I know they’re being used to watch me specifically. Not all the time maybe, but enough. I’ll be near the computer, or writing something after a call, or doing something that later somehow gets referenced indirectly. Then after that, my triage note will get “cleaned up” or “reviewed,” and the wording changes in a way that makes me look careless or unsafe. And people say, “Oh, that’s just routine paperwork,” but it doesn’t feel routine when it keeps happening to me and the edits all lean in the same direction.

So yes, I do feel like those events are directed at me. The camera access, the review comments, the note changes — to me they’re connected. Other people might look at each piece separately and say it’s nothing, but when you’re the one it keeps landing on, it’s hard not to see what’s happening.

So no, I wouldn’t say anyone is putting them there or taking them away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events, actions, or objects in your environment were specifically directed at you, even if others didn’t see the connection?\n\nPatient: Yes. Other people act like they’re just there, like background equipment, but I know they’re being used to watch me specifically. Not all the time maybe, but enough. I’ll be near the computer, or writing something after a call, or doing something that later somehow gets referenced indirectly. And people say, “Oh, that’s just routine paperwork,” but it doesn’t feel routine when it keeps happening to me and the edits all lean in the same direction.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like those events are directed at me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really with TV or strangers, no. I don’t sit at home watching the news thinking they’re talking to me through it or anything like that. It’s more… it’s public in the sense of work, if that makes sense. The station, the cameras, the way supervisors talk around things instead of saying them directly.

Like someone will make a comment that sounds general, like “documentation needs to be tighter” or “we’re reviewing safety concerns,” and other people act like that’s just a normal announcement. But I know when it lines up with my notes being changed or with something I did near the computer earlier, it’s not just general. It’s aimed at me without them saying my name.

And the cameras are the big part of it. People want me to treat them like they’re just part of the building, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through them. Then later my triage notes get altered or “cleaned up,” and somehow the wording makes me look unsafe or careless. They say it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep turning into something that points back at me.

So if you mean random strangers or TV, no, not really. But if you mean things people say or do around the station that look disconnected at first, then yes. I see the connection. Other people may not, or they may pretend not to, but to me it’s pretty clear.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well… they told me I needed to come in after what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I know how the bay is supposed to feel when things are in the right place, you know? The airway bags, the jump kits, the numbers on the dispatch board—there’s a kind of order to it.\n\nI kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern that made sense to me. It wasn’t random. I had to get them lined up the right way, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, because if I stopped before it was finished it felt like something in the whole bay would go wrong. Like the sequence would break.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. I mean, I heard him, I knew he was talking to me, but stepping away felt… unsafe, like I’d be leaving it wrong. So I kept doing it, and I guess that’s why they sent me here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or had racing thoughts that wouldn't stop?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, I don’t think. Like, I’m tired a lot, especially with the shifts, and sometimes my head won’t shut up after a call or after I’ve been trying to get things lined up right, but I wouldn’t say I feel energized in a good way.\n\nThe sleep thing is hard to answer because EMS sleep is already messed up. You get used to sleeping in chunks, waking up fast, always kind of listening. So if you ask whether I’ve needed less sleep and felt great, like I could just keep going and didn’t need it—no, I don’t think so. If I’m not sleeping, it doesn’t feel like extra energy. It feels more like I can’t settle.\n\nRacing thoughts… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It’s more like something keeps repeating or I’m trying to hold the order of things in my head, not like I’m full of ideas or feeling unstoppable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed periods like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things that others around you didn’t seem to notice—like hearing voices, seeing things that others couldn’t see, or believing something strongly that others didn’t share?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not voices or seeing things, not like that. But the believing something part—yes. I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but it’s not just a feeling.\n\nAt the station, we have cameras everywhere, obviously, for safety and liability and all that. I know the injury review people have access, and some of the supervisors too, and I’m pretty sure they’ve been watching me through those cameras. Not just reviewing after something happens—watching. Like checking what I’m doing, when I’m writing, where I’m standing, whether I’m near the computer or the charts.\n\nAnd the triage notes… that’s the part people don’t want to talk about. I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, corrections, documentation cleanup, but that doesn’t make sense when the changes all point in the same direction. It’s like they’re building a file on me.\n\nI’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the changes to your triage notes are being made with the intent to target you? Do you believe someone has told you this directly, or have you come to this conclusion based on patterns you’ve noticed?\n\nPatient: No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.\n\nAnd when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.\n\nAnd the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. And I know how it sounds, but I also know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe these changes to your notes and being watched by supervisors are happening because someone is deliberately trying to harm or undermine you, or do you think it might be due to other factors like misunderstanding or routine procedures?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just misunderstanding. I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, “Maybe it’s routine procedures, maybe you’re stressed, maybe you’re taking it personally.” But if it were routine, it would affect everyone the same way, or at least it would look random. This doesn’t look random.\n\nIt feels deliberate. I’m not saying somebody is going to jump out from behind a door or anything like that, but yes, I think they’re trying to undermine me. Harm me professionally, I guess. Make me look unsafe, unstable, careless—whatever word they need in the file later. Because that’s how these things work. Nobody says it directly. They just build the paper trail until you can’t defend yourself anymore.\n\nThe cameras are part of it because they’re watching how I move around the station, when I’m writing, whether I’m near the computer, what I’m doing after calls. And then the notes change, or comments get made, and it lines up too closely. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep landing on me in a way that makes me look bad.\n\nSo… yes. I think someone, or more than one person, is doing it on purpose. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not entirely your own—like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking them out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… someone putting a thought in my head or pulling one out. I’ve never had it where I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m like, “That thought wasn’t mine,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, and I’ll go over stuff again and again, especially when something doesn’t add up. But that still feels like me thinking it. It’s my own mind trying to make sense of what’s happening. It’s not like an outside voice or some force reaching in and changing my thoughts.\n\nSometimes people make me feel like I’m not allowed to trust my own thinking, if that makes sense. Like they’ll say I’m misreading something or that I’m stressed, and then I start second-guessing how to explain it. But the thoughts themselves feel like mine. So no, I wouldn’t say anyone is putting them there or taking them away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events, actions, or objects in your environment were specifically directed at you, even if others didn’t see the connection?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a lot of what I’ve been trying to explain. It’s not like every random thing in the room feels aimed at me, but certain things at work do, because they connect together in a way other people either don’t notice or don’t want to admit.\n\nLike the station cameras. Other people act like they’re just there, like background equipment, but I know they’re being used to watch me specifically. Not all the time maybe, but enough. I’ll be near the computer, or writing something after a call, or doing something that later somehow gets referenced indirectly. Then after that, my triage note will get “cleaned up” or “reviewed,” and the wording changes in a way that makes me look careless or unsafe. And people say, “Oh, that’s just routine paperwork,” but it doesn’t feel routine when it keeps happening to me and the edits all lean in the same direction.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like those events are directed at me. The camera access, the review comments, the note changes — to me they’re connected. Other people might look at each piece separately and say it’s nothing, but when you’re the one it keeps landing on, it’s hard not to see what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things people say or do in public—like things on TV, in conversations, or even strangers’ actions—have a special meaning for you, even if they don’t seem connected to you at first glance?\n\nPatient: Not really with TV or strangers, no. I don’t sit at home watching the news thinking they’re talking to me through it or anything like that. It’s more… it’s public in the sense of work, if that makes sense. The station, the cameras, the way supervisors talk around things instead of saying them directly.\n\nLike someone will make a comment that sounds general, like “documentation needs to be tighter” or “we’re reviewing safety concerns,” and other people act like that’s just a normal announcement. But I know when it lines up with my notes being changed or with something I did near the computer earlier, it’s not just general. It’s aimed at me without them saying my name.\n\nAnd the cameras are the big part of it. People want me to treat them like they’re just part of the building, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through them. Then later my triage notes get altered or “cleaned up,” and somehow the wording makes me look unsafe or careless. They say it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep turning into something that points back at me.\n\nSo if you mean random strangers or TV, no, not really. But if you mean things people say or do around the station that look disconnected at first, then yes. I see the connection. Other people may not, or they may pretend not to, but to me it’s pretty clear.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But that still feels like me thinking it. It’s my own mind trying to make sense of what’s happening. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s, like, some huge organization outside of work, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying there’s some national thing or strangers following me around. It’s more specific than that.

It’s the injury review people, and some of the supervisors at the station. Not every supervisor, maybe, and not every person in the building, but enough of them that it doesn’t feel like just one person having a problem with me. There’s coordination there. Because one thing happens with the cameras, then later there’s something with my triage notes, and then somebody frames it as “routine paperwork” or “quality review,” but the end result is always the same — it makes me look unsafe, or careless, or like I don’t know what I’m doing.

And that’s the part people keep minimizing. They’ll say, “Well, notes get reviewed all the time,” and yes, I understand that. I’m not stupid. I know documentation gets checked. But when the wording keeps getting changed in a way that points back at me, and when it lines up with them watching me through the station cameras, then it stops being just routine. It’s a smaller set of people, but they have access. That’s what matters. They have the ability to watch, to review, to edit, and to make it look official.

I had to get them lined up the right way, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, because if I stopped before it was finished it felt like something in the whole bay would go wrong. Like the sequence would break.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop and step away, but I couldn’t. Like, I’m tired a lot, especially with the shifts, and sometimes my head won’t shut up after a call or after I’ve been trying to get things lined up right, but I wouldn’t say I feel energized in a good way.\n\nThe sleep thing is hard to answer because EMS sleep is already messed up. You get used to sleeping in chunks, waking up fast, always kind of listening. So if you ask whether I’ve needed less sleep and felt great, like I could just keep going and didn’t need it—no, I don’t think so. If I’m not sleeping, it doesn’t feel like extra energy. It’s more like something keeps repeating or I’m trying to hold the order of things in my head, not like I’m full of ideas or feeling unstoppable. But the believing something part—yes. I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but it’s not just a feeling.\n\nAt the station, we have cameras everywhere, obviously, for safety and liability and all that. I know the injury review people have access, and some of the supervisors too, and I’m pretty sure they’ve been watching me through those cameras. Not just reviewing after something happens—watching. Like checking what I’m doing, when I’m writing, where I’m standing, whether I’m near the computer or the charts.\n\nAnd the triage notes… that’s the part people don’t want to talk about. I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. So… yeah, that’s something I believe pretty strongly, even if other people keep telling me it’s not happening.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the changes to your triage notes are being made with the intent to target you? Do you believe someone has told you this directly, or have you come to this conclusion based on patterns you’ve noticed?\n\nPatient: No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.\n\nAnd when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.\n\nAnd the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. And I know how it sounds, but I also know what I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you believe these changes to your notes and being watched by supervisors are happening because someone is deliberately trying to harm or undermine you, or do you think it might be due to other factors like misunderstanding or routine procedures?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s just misunderstanding. I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, “Maybe it’s routine procedures, maybe you’re stressed, maybe you’re taking it personally.” But if it were routine, it would affect everyone the same way, or at least it would look random. This doesn’t look random.\n\nIt feels deliberate. I’m not saying somebody is going to jump out from behind a door or anything like that, but yes, I think they’re trying to undermine me. Harm me professionally, I guess. Make me look unsafe, unstable, careless—whatever word they need in the file later. Because that’s how these things work. Nobody says it directly. They just build the paper trail until you can’t defend yourself anymore.\n\nThe cameras are part of it because they’re watching how I move around the station, when I’m writing, whether I’m near the computer, what I’m doing after calls. And then the notes change, or comments get made, and it lines up too closely. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep landing on me in a way that makes me look bad.\n\nSo… yes. I think someone, or more than one person, is doing it on purpose. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not entirely your own—like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking them out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not entirely your own—like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking them out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… someone putting a thought in my head or pulling one out. I’ve never had it where I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m like, “That thought wasn’t mine,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, and I’ll go over stuff again and again, especially when something doesn’t add up. But that still feels like me thinking it. It’s my own mind trying to make sense of what’s happening. It’s not like an outside voice or some force reaching in and changing my thoughts.\n\nSometimes people make me feel like I’m not allowed to trust my own thinking, if that makes sense. Like they’ll say I’m misreading something or that I’m stressed, and then I start second-guessing how to explain it. But the thoughts themselves feel like mine. So no, I wouldn’t say anyone is putting them there or taking them away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events, actions, or objects in your environment were specifically directed at you, even if others didn’t see the connection?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a lot of what I’ve been trying to explain. It’s not like every random thing in the room feels aimed at me, but certain things at work do, because they connect together in a way other people either don’t notice or don’t want to admit.\n\nLike the station cameras. Other people act like they’re just there, like background equipment, but I know they’re being used to watch me specifically. Not all the time maybe, but enough. I’ll be near the computer, or writing something after a call, or doing something that later somehow gets referenced indirectly. Then after that, my triage note will get “cleaned up” or “reviewed,” and the wording changes in a way that makes me look careless or unsafe. And people say, “Oh, that’s just routine paperwork,” but it doesn’t feel routine when it keeps happening to me and the edits all lean in the same direction.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like those events are directed at me. The camera access, the review comments, the note changes — to me they’re connected. Other people might look at each piece separately and say it’s nothing, but when you’re the one it keeps landing on, it’s hard not to see what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things people say or do in public—like things on TV, in conversations, or even strangers’ actions—have a special meaning for you, even if they don’t seem connected to you at first glance?\n\nPatient: Not really with TV or strangers, no. I don’t sit at home watching the news thinking they’re talking to me through it or anything like that. It’s more… it’s public in the sense of work, if that makes sense. The station, the cameras, the way supervisors talk around things instead of saying them directly.\n\nLike someone will make a comment that sounds general, like “documentation needs to be tighter” or “we’re reviewing safety concerns,” and other people act like that’s just a normal announcement. It’s aimed at me without them saying my name.\n\nAnd the cameras are the big part of it. Then later my triage notes get altered or “cleaned up,” and somehow the wording makes me look unsafe or careless. They say it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep turning into something that points back at me.\n\nSo if you mean random strangers or TV, no, not really. But if you mean things people say or do around the station that look disconnected at first, then yes. I see the connection. Other people may not, or they may pretend not to, but to me it’s pretty clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people targeting or undermining you are part of a larger group or organization working against you, or is it a smaller, specific set of individuals at the station?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s, like, some huge organization outside of work, if that’s what you mean. Outside of work? Not really in the same way. I mean, I don’t hear something on the radio and think it’s sending me a message, or see people at a grocery store and think they’re involved. That’s not what I’m saying.

What I’m talking about is tied to the station. It’s the injury review people and some supervisors, because they have access to the cameras and the records. They can watch what I’m doing, then later something shows up in my triage notes — wording changed, things “cleaned up,” but always in a way that makes me look unsafe or careless. And then when I question it, they tell me it’s routine paperwork, like I’m supposed to just accept that.

So no, not online or public places or the radio. It’s not random. It’s specific to work, and that’s part of why it bothers me so much, because people try to make it sound like I’m reading into everything, but I’m not. I’m seeing a pattern with the cameras, the reviews, and the notes.

You get used to sleeping in chunks, waking up fast, always kind of listening. So if you ask whether I’ve needed less sleep and felt great, like I could just keep going and didn’t need it—no, I don’t think so. If I’m not sleeping, it doesn’t feel like extra energy. But the believing something part—yes. I know people say I’m reading too much into it, but it’s not just a feeling.\n\nAt the station, we have cameras everywhere, obviously, for safety and liability and all that. I know the injury review people have access, and some of the supervisors too, and I’m pretty sure they’ve been watching me through those cameras. Not just reviewing after something happens—watching. Like checking what I’m doing, when I’m writing, where I’m standing, whether I’m near the computer or the charts.\n\nAnd the triage notes… that’s the part people don’t want to talk about. I’ll put something in the way it happened, and later it looks different, like certain wording is changed so it makes me look careless or unsafe. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, corrections, documentation cleanup, but that doesn’t make sense when the changes all point in the same direction. It’s like they’re building a file on me.\n\nI’ve tried explaining it, but people act like I’m being paranoid or like I just don’t understand the system. I do understand the system. I’ve been doing this long enough to know what routine paperwork looks like. This feels targeted. Do you believe someone has told you this directly, or have you come to this conclusion based on patterns you’ve noticed?\n\nPatient: No one has come up to me and said, “We’re targeting you,” if that’s what you mean. They’re not going to do that. It’s more that I’ve watched the pattern long enough that it’s hard to call it coincidence anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll write a triage note in a certain way because wording matters. You know that. In EMS, if you say someone “refused” versus “declined after explanation,” that changes how it reads. If you leave out that vitals were reassessed, it makes the provider look sloppy. And then later I’ll see the note or the review comments and it’s… shifted. Not always huge things, not something obvious enough that everyone would immediately say, “Oh, that’s tampering.” It’s small edits, small wording changes, but they all lean the same direction. They make it look like I missed something or like my judgment was off.\n\nAnd when I bring it up, they say it’s routine cleanup, quality review, documentation standardization—whatever phrase they’re using that day. But routine changes should be neutral. They shouldn’t always make me look unsafe. That’s the part I can’t get past.\n\nAnd the cameras are part of it too. I know the injury review people and some supervisors can access them. I can feel when I’m being watched—not like a magic thing, I mean because of how people act after. I’ll be standing near the computer or writing something down, and later someone makes a comment that’s just a little too specific, like they already know where I was or what I was doing. So no, nobody told me directly. I came to it from the pattern. I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, “Maybe it’s routine procedures, maybe you’re stressed, maybe you’re taking it personally.” But if it were routine, it would affect everyone the same way, or at least it would look random. This doesn’t look random.\n\nIt feels deliberate. I’m not saying somebody is going to jump out from behind a door or anything like that, but yes, I think they’re trying to undermine me. Harm me professionally, I guess. Make me look unsafe, unstable, careless—whatever word they need in the file later. Because that’s how these things work. Nobody says it directly. They just build the paper trail until you can’t defend yourself anymore.\n\nThe cameras are part of it because they’re watching how I move around the station, when I’m writing, whether I’m near the computer, what I’m doing after calls. And then the notes change, or comments get made, and it lines up too closely. They tell me it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep landing on me in a way that makes me look bad.\n\nSo… yes. I think someone, or more than one person, is doing it on purpose. I don’t know exactly who is calling the shots, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have access, and I think they’re using that access against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your thoughts are not entirely your own—like someone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking them out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not like… someone putting a thought in my head or pulling one out. I’ve never had it where I’m sitting there and suddenly I’m like, “That thought wasn’t mine,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I do get stuck thinking about things, and I’ll go over stuff again and again, especially when something doesn’t add up. But that still feels like me thinking it. It’s my own mind trying to make sense of what’s happening. It’s not like an outside voice or some force reaching in and changing my thoughts.\n\nSometimes people make me feel like I’m not allowed to trust my own thinking, if that makes sense. Like they’ll say I’m misreading something or that I’m stressed, and then I start second-guessing how to explain it. But the thoughts themselves feel like mine. So no, I wouldn’t say anyone is putting them there or taking them away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain events, actions, or objects in your environment were specifically directed at you, even if others didn’t see the connection?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a lot of what I’ve been trying to explain. It’s not like every random thing in the room feels aimed at me, but certain things at work do, because they connect together in a way other people either don’t notice or don’t want to admit.\n\nLike the station cameras. Other people act like they’re just there, like background equipment, but I know they’re being used to watch me specifically. Not all the time maybe, but enough. I’ll be near the computer, or writing something after a call, or doing something that later somehow gets referenced indirectly. Then after that, my triage note will get “cleaned up” or “reviewed,” and the wording changes in a way that makes me look careless or unsafe. And people say, “Oh, that’s just routine paperwork,” but it doesn’t feel routine when it keeps happening to me and the edits all lean in the same direction.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like those events are directed at me. The camera access, the review comments, the note changes — to me they’re connected. Other people might look at each piece separately and say it’s nothing, but when you’re the one it keeps landing on, it’s hard not to see what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things people say or do in public—like things on TV, in conversations, or even strangers’ actions—have a special meaning for you, even if they don’t seem connected to you at first glance?\n\nPatient: Not really with TV or strangers, no. I don’t sit at home watching the news thinking they’re talking to me through it or anything like that. It’s more… it’s public in the sense of work, if that makes sense. The station, the cameras, the way supervisors talk around things instead of saying them directly.\n\nLike someone will make a comment that sounds general, like “documentation needs to be tighter” or “we’re reviewing safety concerns,” and other people act like that’s just a normal announcement. But I know when it lines up with my notes being changed or with something I did near the computer earlier, it’s not just general. It’s aimed at me without them saying my name.\n\nAnd the cameras are the big part of it. People want me to treat them like they’re just part of the building, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through them. Then later my triage notes get altered or “cleaned up,” and somehow the wording makes me look unsafe or careless. They say it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep turning into something that points back at me.\n\nSo if you mean random strangers or TV, no, not really. But if you mean things people say or do around the station that look disconnected at first, then yes. I see the connection. Other people may not, or they may pretend not to, but to me it’s pretty clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people targeting or undermining you are part of a larger group or organization working against you, or is it a smaller, specific set of individuals at the station?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s, like, some huge organization outside of work, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying there’s some national thing or strangers following me around. It’s more specific than that.\n\nIt’s the injury review people, and some of the supervisors at the station. Not every supervisor, maybe, and not every person in the building, but enough of them that it doesn’t feel like just one person having a problem with me. There’s coordination there. Because one thing happens with the cameras, then later there’s something with my triage notes, and then somebody frames it as “routine paperwork” or “quality review,” but the end result is always the same — it makes me look unsafe, or careless, or like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nAnd that’s the part people keep minimizing. They’ll say, “Well, notes get reviewed all the time,” and yes, I understand that. I’m not stupid. I know documentation gets checked. But when the wording keeps getting changed in a way that points back at me, and when it lines up with them watching me through the station cameras, then it stops being just routine. It’s a smaller set of people, but they have access. That’s what matters. They have the ability to watch, to review, to edit, and to make it look official.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events or actions outside of work—like things that happen in public places, on the radio, or online—were directed at you or had a special meaning for you?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Not really in the same way. I mean, I don’t hear something on the radio and think it’s sending me a message, or see people at a grocery store and think they’re involved. That’s not what I’m saying.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is tied to the station. It’s the injury review people and some supervisors, because they have access to the cameras and the records. They can watch what I’m doing, then later something shows up in my triage notes — wording changed, things “cleaned up,” but always in a way that makes me look unsafe or careless. And then when I question it, they tell me it’s routine paperwork, like I’m supposed to just accept that.\n\nSo no, not online or public places or the radio. It’s not random. It’s specific to work, and that’s part of why it bothers me so much, because people try to make it sound like I’m reading into everything, but I’m not. I’m seeing a pattern with the cameras, the reviews, and the notes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things people say or do in public—like things on TV, in conversations, or even strangers’ actions—have a special meaning for you, even if they don’t seem connected to you at first glance?\n\nPatient: Not really with TV or strangers, no. I don’t sit at home watching the news thinking they’re talking to me through it or anything like that. It’s more… it’s public in the sense of work, if that makes sense. The station, the cameras, the way supervisors talk around things instead of saying them directly.\n\nLike someone will make a comment that sounds general, like “documentation needs to be tighter” or “we’re reviewing safety concerns,” and other people act like that’s just a normal announcement. But I know when it lines up with my notes being changed or with something I did near the computer earlier, it’s not just general. It’s aimed at me without them saying my name.\n\nAnd the cameras are the big part of it. People want me to treat them like they’re just part of the building, but I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through them. Then later my triage notes get altered or “cleaned up,” and somehow the wording makes me look unsafe or careless. They say it’s routine paperwork, but routine paperwork doesn’t keep turning into something that points back at me.\n\nSo if you mean random strangers or TV, no, not really. But if you mean things people say or do around the station that look disconnected at first, then yes. I see the connection. Other people may not, or they may pretend not to, but to me it’s pretty clear.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people targeting or undermining you are part of a larger group or organization working against you, or is it a smaller, specific set of individuals at the station?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s, like, some huge organization outside of work, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying there’s some national thing or strangers following me around. It’s more specific than that.\n\nIt’s the injury review people, and some of the supervisors at the station. Not every supervisor, maybe, and not every person in the building, but enough of them that it doesn’t feel like just one person having a problem with me. There’s coordination there. Because one thing happens with the cameras, then later there’s something with my triage notes, and then somebody frames it as “routine paperwork” or “quality review,” but the end result is always the same — it makes me look unsafe, or careless, or like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nAnd that’s the part people keep minimizing. They’ll say, “Well, notes get reviewed all the time,” and yes, I understand that. I’m not stupid. I know documentation gets checked. But when the wording keeps getting changed in a way that points back at me, and when it lines up with them watching me through the station cameras, then it stops being just routine. It’s a smaller set of people, but they have access. That’s what matters. They have the ability to watch, to review, to edit, and to make it look official.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events or actions outside of work—like things that happen in public places, on the radio, or online—were directed at you or had a special meaning for you?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Not really in the same way. I mean, I don’t hear something on the radio and think it’s sending me a message, or see people at a grocery store and think they’re involved. That’s not what I’m saying.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is tied to the station. It’s the injury review people and some supervisors, because they have access to the cameras and the records. They can watch what I’m doing, then later something shows up in my triage notes — wording changed, things “cleaned up,” but always in a way that makes me look unsafe or careless. And then when I question it, they tell me it’s routine paperwork, like I’m supposed to just accept that.\n\nSo no, not online or public places or the radio. It’s not random. It’s specific to work, and that’s part of why it bothers me so much, because people try to make it sound like I’m reading into everything, but I’m not. I’m seeing a pattern with the cameras, the reviews, and the notes.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period lasting several days or more where you felt unusually irritable or elevated, where you might have been easily annoyed or felt like you were on top of the world, even if others didn’t seem to share that view?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean, I get irritable, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it a “period” like that, several days where I’m on top of the world or anything. I don’t really walk around feeling unusually great or like I’m better than everyone.

Annoyed, yes, but that’s usually because something is happening or I’m stressed about something real. So I’d have to think about what you mean by elevated. If you mean, like, energized and happy and unstoppable for days, I don’t think so. If you mean easily irritated, I can be, but I don’t know that it’s in the way you’re asking.

---------- turn 11 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=Qwen/Qwen3-235B-A22B ----------
